维多利亚州首次发现致命 H5N1 禽流感 - SMH.com.au

First detection of deadly H5N1 bird flu confirmed in Victoria - SMH.com.au
新闻简报Victoria has confirmed its first case of the deadly H5N1 bird flu strain after a member of the public reported an ill greater crested tern in Portland. Testing confirmed the bird had contracted the virus, prompting authorities to increase surveillance of wild animal populations and urge poultry keepers to house their birds for at least 14 days. The H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b has been declared ineradicable, with the Australian government committing $113 million to response efforts. While the risk to human health is very low, authorities are concerned about potential impacts on poultry and wildlife, with previous outbreaks resulting in millions of birds being euthanized. Community cooperation is highlighted as crucial for controlling the spread.
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-30。事件日期未明确说明,但检测发生在西澳大利亚州、南澳大利亚州、新南威尔士州和昆士兰州的病例之后。
地点与对象
澳大利亚维多利亚州,特别是靠近南澳大利亚边境的沿海小镇波特兰。受影响的种群包括大凤头燕鸥,对海鸟、水鸟、滨鸟、猛禽以及海豹和海豚等海洋哺乳动物有潜在影响。
事件
确认维多利亚州首次发现H5N1禽流感。一名公众报告了一只生病的鸟,随后证实这只鸟感染了病毒。该事件已得到证实,涉及维多利亚农业部和维多利亚野生动物部等当局。
规模、影响与风险
One confirmed case in a greater crested tern. The virus can devastate poultry and wildlife populations. Risk to human health is stated as very low. Authorities are concerned about potential spread to commercial poultry flocks, with previous outbreaks in 2024 and 2025 resulting in approximately 1.3 million and 600,000 birds euthanized, respectively.
应对、状态与不确定性
Authorities are increasing surveillance of wild animal populations using aerial methods (helicopters and drones). Poultry keepers have been asked to voluntarily house their birds for a minimum of 14 days. H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b has been declared ineradicable, triggering national coordination for cost sharing. The Australian government has committed $113 million to preparations. Community cooperation is emphasized as the most effective control method. No spread to commercial poultry flocks has been reported at this stage.
B routine priorityPublic-health news

First detection of deadly H5N1 bird flu confirmed in Victoria - SMH.com.au

News Brief
Victoria has confirmed its first case of the deadly H5N1 bird flu strain after a member of the public reported an ill greater crested tern in Portland. Testing confirmed the bird had contracted the virus, prompting authorities to increase surveillance of wild animal populations and urge poultry keepers to house their birds for at least 14 days. The H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b has been declared ineradicable, with the Australian government committing $113 million to response efforts. While the risk to human health is very low, authorities are concerned about potential impacts on poultry and wildlife, with previous outbreaks resulting in millions of birds being euthanized. Community cooperation is highlighted as crucial for controlling the spread.
Original source text (English)
Add us as a preferred source on Google Authorities in Victoria are increasing surveillance of wild animal populations after the detection of the state’s first case of the potentially catastrophic new strain of H5N1 bird flu. A member of the public discovered the ill bird in the coastal town of Portland near the border with South Australia and reported it to authorities. Testing on the greater crested tern subsequently confirmed the bird had contracted the virus. The detection comes after a series of cases in Western Australia , South Australia , New South Wales and Queensland . Victoria’s chief veterinary officer Graeme Cooke urged anyone who finds a cluster of at least five sick birds of any species to alert authorities. However, he said even a single sick or dead seabird, waterbird, shorebird or bird of prey – or marine mammal, such as seals and dolphins – should be reported to authorities immediately. “We recognise this can be unsettling news for members of our community,” Cooke said. The virus can devastate poultry and wildlife populations. The latest strain has authorities and farmers on edge amid concerns about an outbreak on poultry farms. The detection of a separate bird flu strain on farms in 2024 resulted in about 1.3 million birds being euthanised, followed by about 600,000 in 2025. Since the detection of the H5N1 virus in Western Australia last month, poultry keepers have been asked to voluntarily house their birds. In Victoria, that period has now been extended to a minimum of 14 days from today. H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b has been declared ineradicable by authorities, following confirmation on Wednesday that the virus is circulating among local birds. The national committee on emergency animal diseases, which comprises state and federal chief veterinary officers, as well as scientific experts, said now the virus had spread among local terns it was not feasible to stamp it out of the country. The official declaration triggers national co-ordination for cost sharing in response to the deadly virus. The Albanese government has already committed $113 million to preparations to deal with an H5N1 incursion, with an emphasis on planning to identify an outbreak swiftly and a response to limit its spread. This includes funding to prepare biosecurity officers to respond to an outbreak in farmed poultry, which may involve quarantining farms or districts and restricting transport between locations. Authorities are using aerial surveillance methods, with helicopters and drones monitoring wildlife populations. Victoria’s chief biodiversity officer James Todd said seal and gannet colonies in Portland were among the wildlife vulnerable to the disease. “We’ve been preparing for H5 arrival in Victoria for a few years now,” he said. Despite the technological response to the virus detection, Todd said co-operation from the community remained the most effective control method at hand. “Our best line of defence at this stage is still the Victorian public.” In a statement, Wildlife Victoria said it received a call from a concerned member of the public earlier this week about the greater crested tern in Portland showing signs of the virus. “Wildlife Victoria monitored the case until Agriculture Victoria responders arrived on scene to contain and test the bird,” it said. “The tern was confirmed positive to H5 bird flu shortly after.” Victorian Farmers Federation president Ryan Milgate said farmers should review their biosecurity plans and look out for unusual illness or unexplained bird deaths. “There has been no spread to commercial poultry flocks or any agricultural environment at this stage, and the risk to human health remains very low,” he said. The signs of H5N1 in birds include lack of co-ordination, tremors, swimming in circles, twisted necks, inability to fly or stand, diarrhoea, breathing difficulties, coughing, sneezing, swelling around the head, neck and eyes as well as cloudy eyes and changes in eye colour, as well as sudden death. No spread to commercial poultry flocks has been reported at this stage.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

禽流感已经蔓延到维多利亚州,更多野生动物死亡迫在眉睫。情况会变得有多糟糕? - 卫报

Bird flu has reached Victoria and more wildlife deaths loom. How bad could it get? - The Guardian
新闻简报Australia has confirmed its first case of H5N1 bird flu in Victoria and detected local transmission in South Australia, marking a concerning new phase in the country's response to the virus. Authorities confirmed the virus in a greater crested tern in Portland and reported 13 new suspected cases in South Australian terns, including five at Kangaroo Island's Seal Bay home to endangered Australian sea lions. With 41 total cases now recorded, experts warn of potential devastating impacts on wildlife, with over 150 bird species and 10 mammal species at 'very high risk' of extinction or major decline. While the risk to human health remains low, concerns grow about potential impacts on the poultry industry. Federal authorities have invested over $113m in preparedness and are focusing on surveillance and conservation efforts, though conservation groups are calling for additional funding to protect vulnerable species.
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026-07-29。事件日期:2026年7月29日(星期四,维多利亚州病例确认)和2026年7月28日(星期三,南澳大利亚州本地传播公告)。
地点与对象
澳大利亚,特别是维多利亚(波特兰)和南澳大利亚(包括袋鼠岛的海豹湾)。受影响的种群包括大凤头燕鸥、濒临灭绝的澳大利亚海狮和其他野生动物物种。
事件
维多利亚州确诊首例 H5N1 禽流感病毒(大凤头燕鸥),并在南澳大利亚鸟类中确诊当地 传播。当局称这是“情况的转变”,也是澳大利亚应对该病毒的新阶段。
规模、影响与风险
澳大利亚共有 41 例病例(有待确认新病例)。超过 150 种本土鸟类和 10 种哺乳动物被评估为灭绝或严重衰退的“极高风险”。对人类健康的风险仍然很低。如果病毒传播到商业鸡群,可能会对家禽业产生重大影响。
应对、状态与不确定性
Authorities focusing on surveillance, site- and species-specific planning, protecting captive breeding populations, and implementing farm biosecurity measures. Federal government has invested over $113m with an additional $24m released. Conservation groups calling for $200m national wildlife resilience fund. Large-scale wildlife deaths not yet occurred but expected to increase.
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird flu has reached Victoria and more wildlife deaths loom. How bad could it get? - The Guardian

News Brief
Australia has confirmed its first case of H5N1 bird flu in Victoria and detected local transmission in South Australia, marking a concerning new phase in the country's response to the virus. Authorities confirmed the virus in a greater crested tern in Portland and reported 13 new suspected cases in South Australian terns, including five at Kangaroo Island's Seal Bay home to endangered Australian sea lions. With 41 total cases now recorded, experts warn of potential devastating impacts on wildlife, with over 150 bird species and 10 mammal species at 'very high risk' of extinction or major decline. While the risk to human health remains low, concerns grow about potential impacts on the poultry industry. Federal authorities have invested over $113m in preparedness and are focusing on surveillance and conservation efforts, though conservation groups are calling for additional funding to protect vulnerable species.
Original source text (English)
Victoria confirmed its first case of the H5N1 bird flu virus on Thursday, in a greater crested tern in Portland. Photograph: DEA/P Jaccod/De Agostini/Getty Images Victoria confirmed its first case of the H5N1 bird flu virus on Thursday, in a greater crested tern in Portland. Photograph: DEA/P Jaccod/De Agostini/Getty Images Bird flu has reached Victoria and more wildlife deaths loom. How bad could it get? Authorities say local transmission of H5 bird flu virus among South Australian birds signals ‘shift in the situation’ Follow our Australia news live blog for latest updates Get our breaking news email , free app or daily news podcast Australia has entered a new phase in dealing with the incursion of deadly H5N1 bird flu , with authorities confirming on Wednesday that local transmission had taken place, and Victoria confirming first case of the highly virulent strain Thursday. “We have had what is suggestive of local transmission for the first time,” Julie Collins, the federal minister for agriculture, fisheries and forestry, said on Wednesday afternoon, after 11 suspected detections were reported in South Australian birds. “We will expect to see increased evidence and deaths of wildlife ... given there has been some local transmission,” she said. “This small shift in the situation will be very disappointing to many, but is certainly not an unexpected development as the last continent to detect H5 bird flu.” On Thursday morning, South Australia reported 13 more cases in greater crested terns. Now that authorities believe transmission of H5 strain is occurring locally, what does this mean for Australia’s wildlife, and how bad could things get? What’s the current status of H5N1 bird flu in Australia? On Thursday, South Australian authorities announced 13 new suspected bird flu detections in greater crested terns, including five cases at Seal Bay on Kangaroo Island, home to a population of endangered Australian sea lions. Victoria also detected its first case of the virus, in a greater crested tern in Portland. Once the new cases are confirmed, that would bring Australia to a total of 41 cases. Julie Collins, the agriculture minister, said the Consultative Committee on Emergency Animal Diseases – comprising federal and state and territory authorities – had met on Wednesday and agreed that “some local transmission has occurred between a number of greater crested terns that are resident in South Australia”. Sign up for the Breaking News Australia email Dr Beth Cookson, Australia’s chief veterinary officer, said genetic sequencing showed all confirmed cases detected to date were related to subantarctic strains of the virus. Clare Scriven, the SA minister for primary industries, said earlier on Wednesday: “This is concerning but not unexpected. We knew that eventually when bird flu arrived in Australia it would spread. It has spread on every other continent around the globe.” What does the arrival of bird flu in Australia mean for poultry and will eggs be more expensive? Read more To date, there have been no H5 detections in poultry or agricultural industries in Australia. “The risk to human health remains low,” Collins said. How could bird flu affect wildlife on Kangaroo Island? Six of the Kangaroo Island bird flu detections have been at Seal Bay, home to the country’s third-largest Australian sea lion population. Conservationists have expressed fears for the sea lion population there. Alexia Wellbelove, campaigns director at the Australian Marine Conservation Society (AMCS), said Australian sea lions were “now on the frontline of the potential wildlife crisis”. About 80% of the Australian sea lion population is found in South Australia. The endangered mammal has seen populations decline by 60% in four decades , with remaining numbers about 12,000. “In South America, this virus killed 30,000 sea lion pups in just six months following transmission from birds to marine mammals,” Wellbelove said. “On our own Heard Island, it killed more than 13,000 elephant seal pups in a single season ... we now have to face the very real possibility of similar devastation here at home, in a species already teetering on the edge.” Scriven said on-beach tours at Seal Bay were temporarily paused to keep people separate from the “very vulnerable” animal population and to reduce the risk of spreading infection. What can be done to stop the spread of bird flu? Not much, unfortunately. Cookson said on Wednesday: “We do know from the overseas experience that once the virus becomes more widespread in wild bird populations it will not be possible to avoid losses in susceptible wildlife in the natural environment.” Authorities had anticipated the arrival of H5N1 in Australia through migratory birds “for a number of years”, she said. They will continue to focus on surveillance, to confirm where the virus is circulating and to “reduce the impacts where possible”. Collins said the federal government had invested more than $113m preparing for the virus’s arrival. “We have also, working with states and territories, now released a further $24m.” Without listing specific animals, Cookson said the government was taking “a number of actions in high-priority conservation species, including site- and species-specific planning”. There would also be a focus on protecting captive breeding populations, and implementing farm biosecurity measures to “limit contact between wild birds and poultry”. Groups including the AMCS and the Invasive Species Council have called on the federal government to establish a $200m national wildlife resilience fund, “focused on emergency protection and recovery for our most at-risk species”. How bad could bird flu get in Australia? Worldwide, the H5N1 variant has caused “unprecedented levels of ongoing outbreaks”, killing millions of wild birds and tens of thousands of mammals. A deadly strain of bird flu has landed on Australian shores. Does it pose a risk to pets? Read more Large-scale deaths have not yet occurred in Australia, but the major concerns relate to potential wildlife impacts: more than 150 native bird species have been assessed as being at “very high risk” of extinction or major decline if they are infected – including the red goshawk, fairy tern, plains-wanderer, shy albatross, black swan and western hooded plover. More than 10 mammal species are at very high risk, including the Australian sea lion, the subantarctic fur seal and the Australian fur seal. There could also be significant impacts on the poultry industry. Globally, an estimated 140m farmed birds have died or been culled since January last year. In the United States, the virus was linked to a 220% spike in egg prices in the first quarter of 2025. Western Australia’s Chamber of Commerce and Industry has warned that if the virus were to reach commercial flocks, it could push egg prices above $10 a carton. Mass culls could be necessary, and the RSPCA has called for fast-tracked research into more humane killing methods, such as the use of nitrogen in gas or foam. Human-to-human transmission of the virus is not common, but since 2003 there have been 1,000 reported cases of H5N1 in humans globally, with a 48% fatality rate . Time
Publication date: 2026-07-29. Event date: Thursday, July 29, 2026 (Victoria case confirmation) and Wednesday, July 28, 2026 (South Australian local transmission announcement).
Location and population
Australia, specifically Victoria (Portland) and South Australia (including Kangaroo Island's Seal Bay). Affected populations include greater crested terns, endangered Australian sea lions, and other wildlife species.
Event
Confirmed first case of H5N1 bird flu virus in Victoria (greater crested tern) and confirmation of local transmission among birds in South Australia. Authorities describe this as a 'shift in the situation' and a new phase in Australia's response to the virus.
Scale, impact and risk
Australia has 41 total cases (pending confirmation of new cases). Over 150 native bird species and 10 mammal species assessed as 'very high risk' of extinction or major decline. Risk to human health remains low. Potential significant impacts on poultry industry if virus reaches commercial flocks.
Response, status and uncertainty
Authorities focusing on surveillance, site- and species-specific planning, protecting captive breeding populations, and implementing farm biosecurity measures. Federal government has invested over $113m with an additional $24m released. Conservation groups calling for $200m national wildlife resilience fund. Large-scale wildlife deaths not yet occurred but expected to increase.

New avian influenza tools for poultry industry on horizon - WATTPoultry.com

B routine priorityPublic-health news

New avian influenza tools for poultry industry on horizon - WATTPoultry.com

News Brief
The U.S. poultry industry is set to benefit from new tools to combat highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI), with over $100 million in research funding awarded by the USDA. Projects focus on diverse approaches: Columbia University is testing far-ultraviolet C light for virus inactivation in barns; St. Jude's is investigating insect vectors and fomites; Kansas State is developing modified mealworms as a vaccine delivery method; and Purdue is exploring an aerosolized antiparasitic, niclosamide. These initiatives aim to provide producers with innovative prevention and treatment options, with preliminary results anticipated in the coming months.
Original source text (English)
New avian influenza tools for poultry industry on horizon Work has started on numerous research projects to help the poultry industry better protect itself from HPAI. Mark Clements July 29, 2026 Kharkhan_Oleg | iStock.com Poultry producers can expect to benefit from numerous new tools over the next few years to help protect their businesses from losses associated with highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI). To help give the industry the options it needs, various research projects are underway in the U.S. with support of the U.S. Department of Agriculture Animal and Plant Inspection Service. Following a competitive process, the agency awarded more than $100 million to a wide range of research projects late last year. The funded projects are designed to advance prevention, therapeutics, vaccine development and research about transmission and potential biosecurity solutions for HPAI. Looking into light At Columbia University, work into far-ultraviolet C light received funding. Among objectives of the study is to determine how susceptible the HPAI virus is to far-ultraviolet C exposure. David Welch, assistant professor of radiation oncology at Columbia University Vagelos College of Physicians and Surgeons and leader of the study, explained: "We already know that far-ultraviolet C is very effective in inactivating viruses such as coronavirus and other influenza viruses, and we expect similar efficacy for avian influenza." The important parameter for efficacy is the dose of ultraviolet light, which is the product of the intensity of the far-ultraviolet C light and the exposure time, he continued. Exposing the virus to an intense source would allow for quicker inactivation. The university is working with a far-ultraviolet C fixture made by biosecurity technology company Visium, designed for use in agricultural settings. Lamps will be installed in a barn filled with chickens at Texas A&M University, with performance monitored over time. The far-ultraviolet C lighting will be fixed to the ceiling throughout the house providing coverage similar to visible lighting. Another project goal is to evaluate the impacts of dust on far-ultraviolet C exposure. The ceiling-mounted lamps will point downward to minimize dust accumulation and protected from water and dust intrusion, which offers the cleaning option of simply spraying them should it be necessary. The far-ultraviolet C light emitted is not within the range visible to birds, meaning the lamps can be left on 24 hours a day to provide continuous protection. Current versions of far-ultraviolet C lamps use excimer bulbs which have an estimated lifetime of at least 11,000 hours — approximately 15 months of continuous operation. Only having the lights on when the barn is in use would extend the time between changing bulbs, Welch noted. Promising advances in light-emitting diode technology will, hopefully, enable far-ultraviolet C light-emitting diodes to become a viable option for these applications, he continued. Insects: Vectors and vaccines Other funded research projects focus on how the virus may be entering the house and could offer producers new tools to protect their houses from the outside. Very local routes of infection are under examination through work being carried out at St. Jude's Children's Research Hospital, where the team of researchers has experience in influenza virology, veterinary medicine and wildlife veterinary medicine. Project leader Richard Webby, member of the Department of Host Microbe, is working with Carol Cordona, professor, Department of Veterinary and Biomedical Sciences at the University of Minnesota Twin Cities, and Justin Brown, associate teaching professor program coordinator, Undergraduate Studies, Veterinary & Biomedical Sciences of Pennsylvania State College of Agricultural Sciences. Among areas being studied is whether and how insects might bring the virus into poultry houses. Traps have already been laid on farms to find out which insects are present. The team will look at how an insect carrying the virus might transmit the infection, for example, by walking over the wet surface of an eye of a bird. The study will also look at the role of songbirds in virus spread. While much work has already been carried out in this field, this is not the case for the currently circulating virus strain. Chukars and pheasants will also be studied to determine why the HPAI virus tends to affect these birds to different degrees. Another avenue of study by the group is the role of fomites, for example dust and water, in HPAI and how the virus behaves when in contact with them. This should result in a better understanding of how the virus might travel from a pond into the poultry house. With work already underway, some results should be available within the next few months and, it is hoped, by project end, it should be possible to inform producers of where they should implement greater insect control and whether greater insect control programs need to be carried out during the bird migration season. While the work at St. Jude's is looking at keeping insects and other vectors out, work at Kansas State University is looking at introducing them in the form of modified mealworms that act as a vaccine delivery method. The modified mealworms could be given as a feed supplement, quickly protecting thousands of birds without the need to handle them. By using gene editing, Kansas State University researchers can quickly update mealworm vaccines to match circulating strains as the virus mutates. Airborne approach Investigation of the aerosolized antiparasitic niclosamide to control and treat HPAI is being carried out at Purdue University. The project is looking at using niclosamide as both a prophylactic and a therapeutic and builds on work carried out at the university in niclosamide use for human health. Niclosamide, currently authorized by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration as an oral anthelmintic medication to primarily treat tapeworm infection, has anti-cancer and antiviral properties. What has held its development back has been its poor solubility. However, thanks to new technology developed by Purdue University professor Yuan Yao's group, its solubility has been increased by more than 5,000 times. Yao, the leading principal investigator in this new work against HPAI, notes that work on niclosamide's use against HPAI began earlier this year and that the university is in the process of building a fogging facility to realize niclosamide's entry into birds' respiratory tracts via water droplets. Niclosamide alters the biological conditions of host cells rather than directly attacking the virus, making infection more difficult. Once chickens are fogged, niclosamide distribution within the respiratory tract will be analyzed to understand how much fogging is needed to achieve sufficient drug distribution for protection. Once parameters are established, HPAI challenge trials will be carried out at a collaborating university. If the research is successful, fogging birds with niclosamide could be used in two different ways, Yao explained. It could be administered therapeutically when disease symptoms appear in a flock or preventatively if a HPAI outbreak occurs on a neighboring farm. 维多利亚州发现首例H5N1禽流感病例 - SMH.com.au

Victoria detects first case of H5N1 bird flu - SMH.com.au
新闻简报澳大利亚维多利亚州报告了首例高致病性 H5N1 禽流感病例,标志着公共卫生领域的重大进展。地方当局证实了这一发现,强调了 禽流感 对动物和人类的潜在威胁。虽然有关受影响个人、接触源和潜在 传播 途径的详细信息仍然有限,但该案例凸显了正在进行的 传播 和生物安全措施的重要性。预计卫生官员将发布有关应对行动和风险评估的进一步信息。H5N1 在维多利亚州的出现提醒人们 禽流感 病毒带来的全球挑战,以及需要警惕监测以防止 暴发 病毒的广泛传播。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Victoria detects first case of H5N1 bird flu - SMH.com.au

News Brief
Victoria, Australia has reported its first case of the highly pathogenic H5N1 bird flu strain, marking a significant public health development. The detection, confirmed by local authorities, underscores the potential threat of avian influenza to both animal and human populations. While details about the affected individual, source of exposure, and potential transmission routes remain limited, this case highlights the importance of ongoing surveillance and biosecurity measures. Health officials are expected to release further information regarding response actions and risk assessment. The emergence of H5N1 in Victoria serves as a reminder of the global challenge posed by avian influenza viruses and the need for vigilant monitoring to prevent widespread outbreaks.

鸡蛋和家禽业对 H5N1 禽流感病毒的传播保持警惕新西兰

Egg and poultry industry on alert for spread of H5N1 bird flu virus - BusinessDesk | NZ
新闻简报新西兰在惠灵顿佩托海滩发现的一只野生棕色贼鸥中确认了首例 H5N1 禽流感病毒病例。虽然当局报告没有证据表明 传播 与 野生鸟类、大规模野生动物死亡或家禽感染之间存在联系,但这一发现促使政府官员和家禽/鸡蛋行业为潜在的传播做好准备。尽管该行业已经准备多年,但该病毒对商业运营和本土鸟类构成了潜在威胁。由于官员们仍在密切关注事态发展,结果仍不确定。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Egg and poultry industry on alert for spread of H5N1 bird flu virus - BusinessDesk | NZ

News Brief
New Zealand has confirmed its first case of the H5N1 bird flu virus in a wild brown skua found on Petone beach in Wellington. While authorities report no evidence of transmission between wild birds, mass wildlife deaths, or poultry infection, the discovery has prompted public officials and the poultry/egg industry to prepare for potential spread. The virus poses a potential threat to commercial operations and native birds, though the industry has been preparing for years. The outcome remains uncertain as officials continue monitoring the situation.

禽流感 - 检测、症状、预防 - 大英百科全书

Bird flu - Detection, Symptoms, Prevention - Encyclopedia Britannica
新闻简报大英百科全书的文章对禽流感进行了全面的概述,重点介绍了检测方法和疫苗的开发。该文章发表于 2026 年 7 月 27 日,讨论了早期发现在预防和控制 暴发 中的重要性。检测方法包括聚合酶链反应(PCR)、病毒抗原检测和病毒培养,并不断开发芯片实验室技术,以快速准确地识别特定亚型。该文章强调了由于该病毒具有进化新毒株的能力,创建有效的 疫苗 所面临的挑战。其中提到 FDA 批准 疫苗 用于 H5N1 亚型,并为未来的 暴发 储备,并不断努力开发更有效的 疫苗,包括基于 mRNA 的 疫苗。文章还指出了 H5N1 病毒对某些 暴发 药物(如金刚烷胺和金刚乙胺)的耐药性,并强调了快速扑杀受感染家禽对于控制 暴发 和减少人类接触的重要性。

Bird flu - Detection, Symptoms, Prevention - Encyclopedia Britannica

News Brief
The article from Encyclopedia Britannica provides a comprehensive overview of avian influenza, focusing on detection methods and vaccine development. Published on July 27, 2026, the article discusses the importance of early detection in preventing and controlling outbreaks. Detection methods include polymerase chain reaction (PCR), viral antigen detection, and viral culture, with ongoing development of lab-on-a-chip technology for rapid and accurate identification of specific subtypes. The article highlights the challenges in creating effective vaccines due to the virus's ability to evolve new strains. It mentions the FDA approval of vaccines for the H5N1 subtype, which were stockpiled for future outbreaks, and ongoing efforts to develop more effective vaccines, including mRNA-based vaccines. The article also notes the resistance of the H5N1 virus to certain antiviral drugs, such as amantadine and rimantadine, and emphasizes the importance of rapid culling of infected poultry to control outbreaks and reduce human exposure.

Contents Ask Anything Health & Medicine Conditions & Diseases Infectious Diseases CITE Share Feedback External Websites Detection of bird flu in bird flu Homework Help Also known as: avian influenza Written and fact-checked by Britannica Editors Encyclopaedia Britannica's editors oversee subject areas in which they have extensive knowledge, whether from years of experience gained by working on that content or via study for an advanced degree.... Britannica Editors Last updated July 30, 2026 •History Britannica AI Ask Anything Table of Contents Table of Contents Ask Anything Early detection of bird flu is important in preventing and controlling outbreaks. One way the virus can be detected is by polymerase chain reaction (PCR), in which nucleic acids from blood or tissue samples are analyzed for the presence of molecules specific to bird flu. Other methods include viral antigen detection, which detects the reaction of antibodies to viral antigens in samples of skin cells or mucus, and viral culture, which is used to confirm the identity of specific subtypes of influenza based on the results of PCR or antigen detection and requires growth of the virus in cells in a laboratory. Tests based on lab-on-a-chip technology that take less than an hour to complete and can accurately identify specific subtypes of bird flu are being developed. This technology consists of a small device (the “chip”) that contains on its surface a series of scaled-down laboratory analyses requiring only a tiny volume of sample (e.g., picolitres of saliva). These chip-based tests, which are portable and cost-effective, can be used to detect different subtypes of influenza in both poultry and humans. Vaccine development Because of the many immunologically distinct viral subtypes that cause influenza in animals and the ability of the virus to rapidly evolve new strains, preparation of effective vaccines is complicated. The most effective control of outbreaks in poultry remains rapid culling of infected farm populations and decontamination of farms and equipment. This measure also serves to reduce the chances for human exposure to the virus. In 2007 the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) approved a vaccine to protect humans against one subtype of the H5N1 virus. It was the first vaccine approved for use against bird flu in humans. It was not intended for commercial use; rather, it was stockpiled to provide some measure of protection against a future outbreak of bird flu. The vaccine also was only moderately effective, necessitating ongoing development of improved agents. Out of those efforts emerged a second vaccine, which used an adjuvant substance to boost the immune response. The vaccine was approved in 2013 by the FDA, and similar to the first vaccine, it was not made commercially available and instead was added to the national stockpile. Scientists continued to work to develop a vaccine that is effective against another subtype of H5N1, as well as a vaccine that might protect against all subtypes of H5N1. In 2023–24, growing concerns about the potential for sustained human-to-human transmission, along with advances in vaccine technology, fueled interest in the development of an mRNA vaccine against H5N1. The introduction of technology based on messenger RNA (mRNA) offered opportunities for the accelerated generation of more effective vaccines. Studies suggest that antiviral drugs developed for human flu viruses would work against bird flu infection in humans. The H5N1 virus, however, appears to be resistant to at least two of the drugs, amantadine and rimantadine. The Editors of Encyclopaedia BritannicaThis article was most recently revised and updated by Kara Rogers. What Is a Second Wave of a Pandemic, and Could It Happen for COVID-19? Introduction References & Edit History Related Topics Images Contents Ask Anything Health & Medicine Conditions & Diseases Respiratory System Diseases CITE Share Feedback External Websites SARS-CoV-2 virus particles Colorized microscopic image of an apoptotic cell (purple) heavily infected with SARS-CoV-2 virus particles (yellow). (more) What Is a Second Wave of a Pandemic, and Could It Happen for COVID-19? Some pandemic diseases re-emerge in different segments of populations and spread anew, giving rise to a second—and sometimes deadlier—wave of disease Homework Help Written by Kara Rogers Kara Rogers is the senior editor of biomedical sciences at Encyclopædia Britannica, where she oversees a range of content from medicine and genetics to microorganisms. She joined Britannica in 2006 and... Kara Rogers Fact-checked by Britannica Editors Encyclopaedia Britannica's editors oversee subject areas in which they have extensive knowledge, whether from years of experience gained by working on that content or via study for an advanced degree.... Britannica Editors History Britannica AI Ask Anything Table of Contents Table of Contents Ask Anything This article was originally published May 15, 2020, and updated October 15, 2020.When a global outbreak of disease begins to slow, feelings of relief and a sense of moving on ensue. But historically, based on past pandemics involving diseases like the plague, cholera, and influenza, that feeling of security has been short-lived. Pandemic diseases, once clear of their initial peak of activity and seemingly on their way out, have a tendency to re-emerge in a different segment of the population and spread anew, giving rise to a second wave of disease.In the modern era, public health experts have warned that highly contagious infectious diseases, including pandemic influenza and coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19), have the potential to re-emerge and produce second and possibly even third waves of disease. And these subsequent rounds of disease are sometimes worse than the first. The influenza pandemic of 1918–19 occurred in three waves, which took place over the course of a year. Whereas the first wave of that pandemic did not deviate noticeably from typical influenza epidemiology, the second and third waves were unusually deadly for 20- to 40-year-olds. The influenza (H1N1) pandemic of 2009 had two waves, the second of which caused significantly more illness than the first.There was much speculation about whether a second wave of disease would sweep the world during the COVID-19 pandemic. To investigate this possibility, scientists developed models of different intervention scenarios and found that the likelihood of an additional round of illness and death by COVID-19 increased significantly when interventions to limit the spread of disease were relaxed. In particular, experts warned, the premature reopening of the economy in areas where new cases of disease continued to be reported all but ensured that a second wave would happen.China, where the COVID-19 outbreak was first detected, was the first country to move past the initial wave of disease, a milestone of relief that came in the first half of February 2020. But, just weeks after newly reported cases dropped to zero in many places in China, researchers became concerned about re-emergence of the disease particularly in areas where restrictions on social distancing and travel had been eased. These concerns were realized in mid-May, when a cluster of new COVID-19 cases was detected in western Wuhan, after a stretch of just 35 days without new infections. Related Topics: pandemic COVID-19 (Show more) See all related content In late September 2020, while countries such as the United States and India were still in their first wave of the COVID-19 pandemic, health officials declared the emergence of a second wave of the disease in Europe. In most European countries, daily cases were higher during the second wave, compared with the first. Australia Confirms `Some Local Spread' of H5N1 - CSIRO Testing An Additional 11 Samples From SA - Avian Flu Diary
新闻简报农业部长朱莉·柯林斯 (Julie Collins) 宣布,澳大利亚已确认南澳大利亚大凤头燕鸥中存在 H5N1 禽流感 本地 传播。这一确认是在 CSIRO 进行测试和测序之后做出的,另外 11 个假定阳性样本已送去进一步分析。确诊病例包括菲尔半岛、约克半岛、袋鼠岛和其他地区的多起野生鸟类。虽然尚未报告大规模死亡事件,但当局预计会在野生动物中进行进一步检测。该病毒株与在南印度洋亚南极地区发现的病毒株相符,并且尚未传播给家禽。目前情况仍在调查中,并已采取积极的 监测 和生物安全措施。

发表日期:2026-07-29。事件日期:未明确说明,但根据CSIRO实验室的检测和测序,确认发生了本地传播。
地点与对象
地区:南澳大利亚 (SA),特别是在弗勒里半岛、约克半岛、袋鼠岛、石灰岩海岸、阿德莱德都会区和东南东南地区的大凤头燕鸥。受影响种群:野生鸟类,包括候鸟和本地海鸟。
事件
据农业部长朱莉·柯林斯 (Julie Collins) 报道,在南澳大利亚大凤头燕鸥中确认了本地 传播 感染 H5N1 禽流感。另外 11 份假定阳性样本正在 CSIRO 接受验证测试。
规模、影响与风险
已确认的检测结果包括南澳大利亚多个地点发现多只 野生鸟类(南方巨海燕、北方巨海燕和大凤头燕鸥)。目前尚无大规模死亡报告,但预计会有进一步检测。没有 跨物种溢出 进入家禽的报道。风险级别:未明确说明,但预计将导致进一步的野生动物检测。
应对、状态与不确定性
联邦科学与工业研究组织 (CSIRO) 正在进行调查,对其他样本进行验证性测试和基因测序。监测 活跃于南澳大利亚。通过农业部长进行官方沟通并更新禽流感门户网站。没有确认家禽 暴发。来源限制:已审查完整的原始文章正文。

Australia Confirms `Some Local Spread' of H5N1 - CSIRO Testing An Additional 11 Samples From SA - Avian Flu Diary

News Brief
Australia has confirmed local transmission of H5N1 avian influenza among greater crested terns in South Australia, as announced by Agriculture Minister Julie Collins. The confirmation follows testing and sequencing by CSIRO, with 11 additional presumed positive samples sent for further analysis. Confirmed cases include multiple wild birds across the Fleurieu Peninsula, Yorke Peninsula, Kangaroo Island, and other regions. While no mass mortalities have been reported, authorities expect further detections in wildlife. The virus strain matches one detected in the Southern Indian Ocean Sub-Antarctic Territories and has not spread to poultry. The situation remains under investigation with active surveillance and biosecurity measures in place.
Original source text (English)
" I can advise that today the consultative committee on animal diseases, emergency animal diseases met and has agreed based on testing and sequencing by the CSRO lab that some local transmission has occurred between a number of greater crested terns that are resident in South Australia. While there remains no evidence of any mass mortalities at this point in time, we do expect with this local transmission that there will be further detections in Australian wildlife. " - From Transcript of remarks by Agriculture Minister Julie Collins #19,267 Overnight South Australia updated their avian flu web portal , announcing that another 11 presumed positive samples have been sent to CSIRO for confirmation and genetic testing. Current situation South Australia’s first confirmed detection of H5 bird flu was in a wild migratory bird (southern giant petrel) on Wednesday, 24 June at Knights Beach at Port Elliot on the Fleurieu Peninsula. Further confirmed detections include a giant petrel (8 July at Hardwicke Bay, Yorke Peninsula); a southern giant petrel (10 July at Emu Bay, Kangaroo Island); a northern giant petrel (10 July at Port Vincent, Yorke Peninsula); three greater crested terns (10 July at Robe, Limestone Coast; 26 July at Semaphore, metro Adelaide; and 26 July at Robe, Limestone Coast); and seven greater crested terns (28 July at Cape Jaffa, Port MacDonnell and Southend, Lower South East). A further 11 suspected H5 bird flu detections are undergoing further confirmatory testing at the Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness in Victoria. The strain of virus is consistent with the strain detected in the Southern Indian Ocean Sub-Antarctic Territories, including Heard Island. This strain has not been detected in poultry and has not become established in any part of Australia. Up-to-date national information is available at birdflu.gov.au . This comes just 1 day after CSIRO confirmed 7 new cases in local seabirds from the state's southeast Limestone Coast. The results of those tests also strongly suggest local transmission of the virus has occurred among native birds. Although disappointing, none of this is particularly surprising. The history with HPAI H5 - particularly since the emergence and subsequent evolution of the 2.3.4.4x clade - has been that it travels well in migratory birds, and that when it reaches new territories, it often finds suitable native hosts. While the situation in Australia continues to evolve, so far there are no reports of spillovers into poultry. On Monday I shared an X/Twitter post by Professor Raina Macintyre showing the recent H5N1 detections overlaid on a map of Australia's poultry production regions. Yesterday, the above mentioned data was published in a preprint on bioRxiv . HPAI H5N1 risk in Australia: a model for the prediction of poultry outbreaks Pan Zhang, Samsung Lim, Ashley Quigley, C Raina MacIntyre doi: https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.07.27.740638 This article is a preprint and has not been certified by peer review Preview PDF Abstract The panzootic highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) H5N1 virus has now been detected on the Australian mainland, with incursions from the sub-Antarctic region posing an increasing threat to domestic wildlife and poultry populations. Our study aimed to predict the risk of HPAI H5N1 poultry outbreaks across Australia at the local government area (LGA) level using a range of influential risk factors. We first used a Maximum Entropy (MaxEnt) model to estimate the environmental suitability for HPAI H5N1 occurrence across Australia. The resulting suitability layer was then integrated with five additional predictor layers, including abundance data for two Southern Ocean wild birds, one of which has introduced HPAI H5N1 into Australia; abundance data for 28 native Australian wild birds; native bird flyways across Australia; Australian chicken density; and poultry farm density. The six layers were aggregated and averaged to generate an HPAI H5N1 risk map for poultry outbreaks across Australian LGAs. Although most incursions have occurred in Western Australia (WA) and South Australia (SA), we identified New South Wales (NSW) and Victoria (VIC) as having the highest predicted risk of HPAI H5N1 poultry outbreaks . Additional high-risk areas were identified in WA, SA, and Tasmania (TAS) . In contrast, the Northern Territory (NT) and large parts of Queensland (QLD), WA, and SA were predicted to be at low risk. These findings provide a spatially explicit framework to support targeted surveillance, preparedness, and biosecurity measures aimed at mitigating the impact of future HPAI H5N1 outbreaks in Australian poultry. The Australian government has provided a number of resources for both commercial and backyard poultry producers to help improve their biosecurity

新西兰首例 H5N1 病例对野生动物和农民意味着什么 - 新西兰先驱报

What New Zealand’s first H5N1 case means for wildlife and farmers - NZ Herald
新闻简报新西兰确认了首例 高致病性禽流感 病毒 H5N1 病例,这是一个重要但令人期待的里程碑。该病毒已在野生动物中检测到,包括海鸟和极度濒危物种,但目前的检测率仍然很低。当局一直在通过加强 监测、诊断能力和机构间合作来为此次活动做准备。应对工作的重点是确定感染程度、加强 监测 和实施生物安全措施。疫苗 已获得批准,并正在为濒临灭绝的 野生鸟类 部署,但其实际应用仅限于圈养或管理种群。传播 在人口密集的群体中的风险最高,可能会影响其他物种和家畜。基因组测序将在追踪病毒的起源和传播方面发挥至关重要的作用。新西兰现在的首要任务是快速发现和管理新的入侵,利用国际经验尽量减少对野生动物和农业的影响。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-27。证据中未明确报告事件日期。
地点与对象
受影响的种群包括野生动物(棕贼鸥、大凤头燕鸥、野生鸟类 的极度濒危物种,如鸮鸮、鸮鹦鹉、黑长脚鹬、滨鸻和红冠鸻、海豹和海狮)以及可能的家禽和牲畜。
事件
新西兰首次检测到 高致病性禽流感 病毒 H5N1,这是一个重要的里程碑,但并非意外。该事件是根据在野生动物中检测到 H5N1 的证据而得到证实的。
规模、影响与风险
目前检测率很低。该病毒已在个别海鸟而非广泛分布的野生动物 死亡率 中检测到。传播 的风险在生活在大型群体中的群体中最大,特别是在高密度群体中,这可能是其他物种和潜在家畜的 传播 的来源。
应对、状态与不确定性
新西兰根据国际经验,一直在为H5N1的到来做好准备,并加强了监测计划、诊断能力和机构之间的合作。优先事项包括确定感染的规模和分布、增加 监测、测试可能受影响的野生动物以及共享信息。加强生物安全措施和公众沟通至关重要。疫苗 已被批准用于极度濒危的 野生鸟类 物种,并正在更大规模地推广。基因组测序对于追踪病毒非常重要。

What New Zealand’s first H5N1 case means for wildlife and farmers - NZ Herald

News Brief
New Zealand has confirmed its first case of the highly pathogenic avian influenza virus H5N1, marking a significant but anticipated milestone. The virus has been detected in wildlife, including seabirds and critically endangered species, with current detection rates remaining low. Authorities have been preparing for this event by strengthening surveillance, diagnostic capabilities, and interagency collaboration. Response efforts focus on determining the extent of infection, enhancing surveillance, and implementing biosecurity measures. A vaccine has been approved and is being deployed for endangered wild birds, though its practical application is limited to captive or managed populations. The risk of transmission is highest in densely populated colonies, potentially affecting other species and domestic animals. Genomic sequencing will play a crucial role in tracking the virus's origin and spread. New Zealand's priority is now to quickly detect and manage new incursions, leveraging international experience to minimize impacts on wildlife and agriculture.
Original source text (English)
From alert to action: How New Zealand is responding to H5N1’s arrival Opinion by Jemma Geoghegan & Nigel French NZ Herald· 4 mins to read Jemma Geoghegan is Professor and Webster Family Chair in Viral Pathogenesis, University of Otago; Nigel French is Distinguished Professor of Infectious Disease Epidemiology and Public Health, Te Kunenga ki Pūrehuroa – Massey University Subscribe to listen Access to Herald Premium articles require a Premium subscription. Subscribe now to listen. For now, detection rates are low, but ongoing surveillance of H5N1 will be essential to determine whether additional cases emerge. Photo / Getty Images The first cases of the highly pathogenic avian influenza virus H5N1 in New Zealand mark an important milestone, but not an unexpected one. Since the virus reached Australia last month, and as it continued its global spread, scientists and government agencies have been preparing for the possibility that itwould eventually arrive here. For now, detection rates are low, but ongoing surveillance of H5N1 will be essential to determine whether additional cases emerge. Australia provides an important indication of what New Zealand may face. H5N1 was detected in a brown skua in Western Australia less than four weeks ago. Further detections followed in Western Australia and South Australia, mainly in migratory seabirds, with a detection subsequently reported in New South Wales. More recently, H5N1 was confirmed in a greater crested tern in South Australia, marking the first detection in a resident Australian seabird, and therefore a possible indication of local transmission. So far, the Australian detections have involved individual seabirds rather than widespread wildlife mortality. The virus has not been detected in poultry in Australia. Susceptibility to infection and severe disease varies between species and individual incursions in solitary scavenging or predatory seabirds may not lead to widespread infection. The risk of transmission is likely greatest in populations that live in large colonies, particularly at high density. These can be a source of transmission to other species that inhabit the same environments — for example, shorebirds and marine mammals such as seals and sea lions. Proximity to backyard poultry and other livestock populations then becomes critical for onward transmission to domestic animals. How prepared is New Zealand? The advantage for New Zealand is that we are not facing this blindly. The experiences of Australia and the rest of the world have provided several years of information about how H5N1 behaves, which species may be most susceptible and which surveillance and response approaches are most effective. New Zealand has also spent recent years strengthening surveillance programmes, diagnostic capacity and collaboration between wildlife, agricultural and public health agencies. These systems will now be tested in practice. No country can be completely prepared for a virus with such a broad host range and capacity to move over long distances in wild birds. However, early detection, rapid information sharing and clear co-ordination between agencies will give New Zealand the best opportunity to understand and reduce the impacts of the virus. Priorities for the response The immediate focus should be on determining the scale and distribution of infection. This means increasing surveillance around the initial detection, testing potentially affected wildlife, and rapidly sharing information between laboratories, wildlife managers, veterinarians, public health authorities and affected communities. Enhanced biosecurity measures and clear public communication will also be essential, building on preparedness plans developed by the Ministry for Primary Industries, the Department of Conservation, Health New Zealand and regional authorities. The Department of Conservation has trialled a vaccine developed for poultry on five of our critically endangered taonga species of wild bird (takahē, kākāpō, black stilts, shore plovers and red-crowned kākāriki). The vaccine is safe and results in an immune response that is likely to be protective. As a consequence, the Ministry for Primary Industries has approved the vaccine for use in these species, and this is now being rolled out on a larger scale. However, it requires two doses and can only be practically implemented in captive, managed populations or on offshore islands. The devastating impact of the virus on marine animals, such as the northern and southern elephant seals, reminds us of the vulnerability of New Zealand populations of seals and sea lions, including the pakake/New Zealand sea lion. In the US, trials of a bird flu vaccine formulated for cattle have been carried out in the endangered Hawaiian monk seal. While the trials show promise, we are a long way from using these in marine mammals in Aotearoa. Genomic sequencing will be particularly important. It can help identify where the virus likely originated, whether New Zealand has experienced one or several introductions, and whether transmission is occurring locally. It may also help link detections in New Zealand with outbreaks in Australia or elsewhere along regional bird-migration routes. The arrival of H5N1 is unlikely to be the end of the story. International experience suggests further incursions may occur as migratory birds continue to move through the region. The challenge therefore shifts from trying to prevent the virus from arriving to detecting new incursions quickly and managing their consequences. The coming weeks will be important in establishing whether this is an isolated detection or the first sign of wider circulation. New Zealand has the benefit of advance warning and extensive international experience. The priority now is to put those lessons into action

H5N1 Bird Flu Spreads to Queensland Shores - Devdiscourse

News Brief
Queensland, Australia's fourth state, has confirmed its first case of H5N1 bird flu in a migratory seabird found on Moreton Island. This makes Queensland the fourth state in Australia to report cases, joining New South Wales, Victoria, and South Australia. Authorities have confirmed that while the virus has been detected in 18 cases across four states, there is no evidence of large-scale animal deaths, and the risk to human health remains low. The announcement comes as authorities continue to monitor the situation closely.

Local transmission of H5N1 declared as alarm sounds for local birds, sea lions - SMH.com.au
新闻简报Australia has declared local transmission of the deadly H5N1 bird flu in South Australia, with 11 cases detected among greater crested terns. The virus, likely brought by migratory seabirds, poses a significant threat to wildlife, including endangered sea lions on Kangaroo Island. Federal and state officials acknowledge the virus will spread in local populations, with little that can be done to contain it in the wild. Conservation groups are urging immediate deployment of a $200 million fund to boost wildlife resilience, while the government has committed $113 million for response planning and environmental measures. The outbreak raises concerns about potential extinction risks for vulnerable species, though no cases have been detected in farmed poultry.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Local transmission of H5N1 declared as alarm sounds for local birds, sea lions - SMH.com.au

News Brief
Australia has declared local transmission of the deadly H5N1 bird flu in South Australia, with 11 cases detected among greater crested terns. The virus, likely brought by migratory seabirds, poses a significant threat to wildlife, including endangered sea lions on Kangaroo Island. Federal and state officials acknowledge the virus will spread in local populations, with little that can be done to contain it in the wild. Conservation groups are urging immediate deployment of a $200 million fund to boost wildlife resilience, while the government has committed $113 million for response planning and environmental measures. The outbreak raises concerns about potential extinction risks for vulnerable species, though no cases have been detected in farmed poultry.
Original source text (English)
Add us as a preferred source on Google Dreaded local transmission of the deadly H5N1 bird flu has been declared in South Australia, sounding the alarm for avian species around the country and sparking fears for a colony of endangered sea lions, which are also susceptible to the virus. The South Australian government said on Wednesday that 11 new cases of the virus had been detected among greater crested terns, a local species that does not leave the country. CSIRO national laboratory testing underway is expected to confirm the SA results. It is likely that migratory seabirds from the Antarctic brought the virus to Australia and it then spread among terns. Seven detections were made on South Australia’s south-east coast, and four on Kangaroo Island. SA Primary Industries Minister Clare Scriven said results showed it was “very, very likely” the birds were infected with H5N1 virus. Federal Agriculture Minister Julie Collins said the virus was believed to be spreading in terns and that with the onset of local transmission, it would spread in local populations. “We do expect with this local transmission there will be further transmission in Australian wildlife,” Collins said. Australia’s chief veterinary officer, Dr Beth Cookson, said little could be done to stem the virus moving around the country. “In the natural environment there are limited feasible actions to contain the spread,” Cookson said. Bird flu viruses have circulated around the globe for decades. H5N1 is a particularly deadly variant and has killed hundreds of millions of birds around the world. It is believed that an outbreak in Australia could spark an environmental disaster, risking extinction of dozens of species including marine mammals such as otters, dolphins and seals that also suffer from infection. Scriven said popular beach tours to the colony of about 1000 sea lions at Seal Bay on Kangaroo Island had been halted. The virus has not been detected among the marine mammals, but tours were halted to stop any potential disturbances that could harm the colony. There are also about 100,000 long-nosed fur seals on or around Kangaroo Island. These animals can also contract the virus. “Everyone knows how susceptible the [sea lion] population is, and they are an endangered species,” Scriven said. H5N1 arrived in South America in 2022 and killed more than 30,000 South American sea lions, 17,000 southern elephant seal pups and unknown numbers of porpoises, dolphins and otters, as well as at least 650,000 native birds. More than 13,000 seal pups were killed by the virus on Australia’s sub-Antarctic Heard Island territory, which was more than three-quarters of new births on the island. Conservation groups are calling for the immediate deployment of a $200 million fund to boost wildlife resilience. They argue that while the virus cannot be stopped from spreading among wildlife, investment in habitat restoration and programs to cull feral predators can help native animals survive the outbreak. “With only 12,000 individuals left, the Australian sea lion simply cannot absorb the kind of losses we have seen bird flu inflict on marine mammals overseas,” said Australian Marine Conservation Society campaign director Alexia Wellbelove. “Every colony, every pup, every individual matters when a population is this small. We cannot afford to wait.” Invasive Species Council senior advocate James Johnston said emergency wildlife funds were critical. “We can’t stop bird flu reaching every corner of Australia, but we can give our wildlife a much better chance of surviving it,” he said. The Albanese government committed $113 million to preparations to deal with an H5N1 incursion, with an emphasis on planning to identify an outbreak swiftly and a response to limit its spread. This includes funding to prepare biosecurity officers to respond to an outbreak in farmed poultry, which may involve quarantining farms or districts and restricting transport between locations. Tens of millions of dollars have also been committed to environmental measures such as building pup shelters on Kangaroo Island to help reduce infant mortality in sea lions, removing weeds from islands to improve habitat for seabirds, and funding pre-existing captive breeding programs for endangered species such as the Tasmanian devil, orange-bellied parrot and regent honeyeater. The funds would also pay for teams to remove carcasses of infected animals swiftly to prevent further spread and limit contact between wild and farmed birds. Australia’s unique wildlife, which has evolved in isolation for millions of years, could be particularly vulnerable to H5N1. But little is known about native species’ vulnerability. There have been no detections of the virus in farmed poultry. An outbreak would cause a significant impact on chicken meat and egg production. H5N1 in the United States has caused more than 170 million birds to be culled since 2022, while egg prices had tripled by 2025. H5N1 is not considered a significant risk to human health. However, pet birds and cats usually become extremely ill and die when infected. Dogs are more resistant to the virus and rarely die or become severely ill. The same is believed to be true for rabbits and guinea pigs as well as cows, sheep, pigs and horses. People are encouraged to look out for possible infections in birds and marine mammals. Signs include sudden death or difficulty breathing, standing, walking or flying. Sick animals should not be touched, but their location should be noted, with photos and videos, before calling the bird flu hotline on 1800 675 888. 现已正式公布:四个州出现禽流感病例 - 澳大利亚博物馆

It's now official: bird flu cases in four states - Australian Museum
新闻简报澳大利亚昆士兰州确认了首例 高致病性禽流感(H5 禽流感)病例,自 6 月 20 日在西澳大利亚首次出现以来,现已在四个州检测到该病毒。最新病例涉及莫顿岛发现的一只海燕,这是全国第 17 例确诊病例。尽管对人类健康的风险仍然很低,但当局警告称,这可能会给当地野生动物带来灾难性后果,脆弱物种可能会濒临灭绝。家禽养殖户正在为最坏的情况做准备,但尚未发现家禽或大规模动物死亡的报道。联邦和州政府正在升级 监测 并与业界合作遏制该病毒,借鉴过去根除 H7 暴发 的成功经验。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

It's now official: bird flu cases in four states - Australian Museum

News Brief
Australia has confirmed its first case of highly pathogenic avian influenza (H5 bird flu) in Queensland, with the virus now detected in four states since its initial appearance in Western Australia on June 20. The latest case involved a petrel found on Moreton Island, marking the 17th confirmed detection nationwide. While the risk to human health remains low, authorities warn of potential catastrophic consequences for native wildlife, with vulnerable species potentially pushed closer to extinction. Poultry farmers are preparing for the worst, though no detections in poultry or large-scale animal deaths have been reported. Federal and state governments are escalating surveillance and working with the industry to contain the virus, drawing on past success in eradicating H7 outbreaks.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Skip to acknowledgement of country Skip to footer Australian Museum Australian Museum website What's On AM Shop Join & Give Search Menu Home AAP News It's now official: bird flu cases in four states It's now official: bird flu cases in four states Australian General News (Photo: Darren England/AAP PHOTOS) Published 25 July 2026 Author Robyn Wuth and John Kidman Located Brisbane Source Australian Associated Press A petrel found washed up on a coastal island by wildlife rangers has been confirmed positive for the deadly H5 bird flu strain. It's discovery, 75 minutes north by ferry from Brisbane on Moreton Island, is a Queensland first. The highly pathogenic avian influenza has now been detected in four states after being found in Western Australia, South Australia and NSW. The most recent detection involved a petrel washing up on a beach on Queensland's Moreton Island. (Tracey Ferrier/AAP PHOTOS) In all there have been 17 confirmed detections since the disease was first reported in WA on June 20. The latest discovery prompted warnings in Queensland on Friday that the virus could have catastrophic consequences for native wildlife and push vulnerable species closer to extinction. Poultry farmers are also battening down the hatches as the virus spreads. However Department of Primary Industry officials said on Saturday there remained no detections in poultry and nor was there evidence of large-scale deaths in any animals in the state. The risk to human health remains low. Poultry farmers are preparing for the worst as the virus spreads. (Jono Searle/AAP PHOTOS) Queensland Chief Veterinary Officer Dr Allison Crook said the confirmed case had been expected. "We have known that a H5 bird flu detection in Queensland was a matter of when, not if," she said. "We have further escalated our surveillance program ... and are delivering heightened H5 bird flu awareness and engagement activities." Federal Agriculture Minister Julie Collins said on Friday the Commonwealth was chiming in with the poultry industry in a bid to keep the "very virulent" and "highly contagious" strain at bay. Julie Collins says the federal government is doing what it can to keep the virus at bay. (Lukas Coch/AAP PHOTOS) "We've had two outbreaks of the H7 bird flu virus in Australia in the last five years or so, and we've been able to successfully eradicate those by working with industry and governments, state and federal," she noted. That aside, Invasive Species Council policy director Dr Carol Booth told AAP bird flu spread should alarm anyone who cared about native animals. Australia's wildlife was already under intense pressure and H5 bird flu could "tip quite a lot of them much closer to extinction", she said. © 2026 AAP View AAP content Terms & Conditions. Time
Published 25 July 2026; Event date: First reported in WA on June 20, 2026.
Location and population
Australia (Queensland, Western Australia, South Australia, NSW); Affected population: Wildlife (petrel), poultry farmers.
Event
Confirmed detection of highly pathogenic avian influenza (H5 bird flu strain) in a petrel on Moreton Island, Queensland; First detection in Queensland; Total 17 confirmed detections across four states since June 20.
Scale, impact and risk
17 confirmed detections; No detections in poultry or large-scale animal deaths reported; Risk to human health remains low; Potential catastrophic consequences for native wildlife and vulnerable species.
Response, status and uncertainty
Queensland has escalated surveillance program and heightened awareness activities; Federal government working with poultry industry to contain the virus; Previous H7 outbreaks successfully eradicated; No evidence of large-scale deaths in animals; Source limitation: Full original article body reviewed.

澳大利亚昆士兰州确认第四例 H5N1 病例 - The Poultry Site

Australia confirms fourth case of H5N1 in Queensland - The Poultry Site
新闻简报澳大利亚在昆士兰州确认了第四例 H5N1 禽流感 病例,使检测总数达到 18 例,但尚未确认家禽感染。该案件于 2026 年 7 月 27 日报告,当局维持 监测 和监控工作。目前情况仍在调查中,目前尚未报告对家禽生产产生直接影响。这一事态发展凸显了 H5N1 在本地鸟类种群中的持续传播,而家禽业迄今为止尚未受到影响。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Australia confirms fourth case of H5N1 in Queensland - The Poultry Site

News Brief
Australia has confirmed its fourth case of H5N1 avian influenza in Queensland, bringing the total detections to 18, though no poultry infections have been confirmed. The case was reported on July 27, 2026, with authorities maintaining surveillance and monitoring efforts. The situation remains under investigation, with no immediate impact on poultry production reported. This development highlights the continued spread of H5N1 in native bird populations while the poultry industry remains unaffected so far.
Original source text (English)

随着新病例确诊,致命禽流感再次蔓延 - thenightly.com.au

Deadly bird flu spreads again as new case confirmed - thenightly.com.au
新闻简报澳大利亚正在努力应对致命的 H5N1 禽流感病毒的传播,当局在波特兰附近的维多利亚州确认了一个新病例。在一只大凤头燕鸥中发现了 高致病性禽流感,这标志着该病毒沿着澳大利亚南部海岸线的传播又迈出了一步。此前,最近在南澳大利亚、西澳大利亚、新南威尔士和昆士兰发现了涉及 野生鸟类 的事件。该病毒于六月首次抵达澳大利亚,此后通过迁徙海鸟传播。虽然目前的病例仅限于野生动物,但由于对本地物种和家禽业的潜在威胁,官员们仍保持警惕。目前还没有人类感染的报告,生物安全机构将继续密切监测情况。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表日期:2026-07-30。事件日期:未明确说明,但报道为'20小时前'和'更新于19小时前'(相对于发表日期)
地点与对象
地区:维多利亚州,特别是该州西南部波特兰附近。受影响种群:野生鸟类,包括大凤头燕鸥。
事件
确认在维多利亚州波特兰附近的一只大凤头燕鸥中检测到高致病性 H5N1 禽流感 菌株。维多利亚农业报道。这标志着病毒在澳大利亚南部海岸线的传播。
规模、影响与风险
病例:维多利亚州、南澳大利亚州、西澳大利亚州、新南威尔士州和昆士兰州的 野生鸟类 检测到多个 H5N1。死亡人数:未具体说明。传播:在南澳大利亚大凤头燕鸥中确认了当地的 传播。风险:由于担心该病毒威胁澳大利亚本土野生动物和家禽业,官员们仍保持高度警惕。
应对、状态与不确定性
回应:当局正在监测病毒的传播,生物安全机构也参与其中。不确定性:目前的检测仅限于野生鸟类,但人们担心可能传播到家禽。没有提及有关人类病例或具体控制措施的信息。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Deadly bird flu spreads again as new case confirmed - thenightly.com.au

News Brief
Australia is grappling with the spread of the deadly H5N1 bird flu strain, with authorities confirming a new case in Victoria near Portland. The highly pathogenic avian influenza was detected in a greater crested tern, marking another step in the virus's movement along Australia's southern coastline. This follows recent detections in South Australia, Western Australia, New South Wales, and Queensland, all involving wild birds. The virus first arrived in Australia in June and has since spread through migratory seabirds. While current cases are confined to wildlife, officials remain vigilant due to the potential threat to native species and the poultry industry. No human infections have been reported, and biosecurity agencies continue to monitor the situation closely.
Original source text (English)

Location and population
Region: Victoria, specifically near Portland in the state's south-west. Affected population: Wild birds, including a greater crested tern.
Event
Confirmed detection of the highly pathogenic H5N1 avian influenza strain in a greater crested tern near Portland, Victoria. Reported by Agriculture Victoria. This marks the spread of the virus across Australia's southern coastline.

Cases: Multiple H5N1 detections in wild birds across Victoria, South Australia, Western Australia, New South Wales, and Queensland. Deaths: Not specified. 南澳大利亚州发现 11 例新疑似病例后,H5 禽流感病毒可能在当地传播 - Anadolu Ajansı

South Australia says H5 bird flu strain likely spreading locally after 11 new suspected cases identified - Anadolu Ajansı
新闻简报南澳大利亚州当局在野生鸟类发现了11例新的H5禽流感疑似病例,其中7例位于该州东南部,4例位于袋鼠岛。包括第一产业部长克莱尔·斯克里文(Clare Scriven)在内的官员表示,该病毒“非常非常有可能”正在当地传播,尽管联邦科学与工业研究组织(CSIRO)实验室的最终测试结果仍在等待中。商业家禽中尚未发现病例。为了降低风险,海豹湾已暂时关闭,以保护当地海狮种群。南澳大利亚首例确诊病例于 6 月 14 日报告。当局预计实验室结果将在周三或周四公布,以核实当地 传播 之间的情况。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026 年 7 月 29 日。事件日期:未明确说明,但周三发现了 11 起新疑似病例,预计 CSIRO 实验室结果将于周三或周四出炉。
地点与对象
地区:南澳大利亚。具体地区:东南部(7例)和袋鼠岛(4例)。受影响人群:野生鸟类。没有商业家禽受到影响。
事件
南澳大利亚当局报告了 11 起新的 H5 禽流感疑似病例,并表示该病毒“非常非常有可能”在当地传播。这些病例是在 野生鸟类 中发现的,但在商业家禽中没有发现。该活动是初步的,等待最终测试结果。
规模、影响与风险
病例:新增疑似病例11例。死亡人数:未报告。暴露情况:未报告。传播:可能在 野生鸟类 之间定位 传播。对健康系统的影响:未报告。运营效果:海豹湾暂时关闭,以保护海狮种群。风险:官方表示“极有可能”代表局部分布。
应对、状态与不确定性
调查:正在进行中,预计 CSIRO 实验室将在周三或周四得出最终测试结果。测试:等待 CSIRO 实验室的确认。隔离:未报告。接触者追踪:不适用。预防措施:海豹湾不对公众开放。治疗:未报道。官方通讯:第一产业部长克莱尔·斯克里文(Clare Scriven)发表声明。目前状态:初步确定,等待确认。未解决的信息:疾病是否在 野生鸟类 之间局部传播。源体限制:无,因为 content_status 已满。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

South Australia says H5 bird flu strain likely spreading locally after 11 new suspected cases identified - Anadolu Ajansı

News Brief
South Australian authorities have identified 11 new suspected cases of H5 bird flu strain in wild birds, with seven cases in the state's South East and four on Kangaroo Island. Officials, including Primary Industries Minister Clare Scriven, stated it is "very, very likely" that the virus is spreading locally, though final testing results from the CSIRO lab are pending. No cases have been detected in commercial poultry. To mitigate risks, Seal Bay has been temporarily closed to protect the local sea lion population. The first confirmed case in South Australia was reported on June 14. Authorities expect lab results by Wednesday or Thursday to verify local transmission between wild birds.
Original source text (English)
Asia - Pacific South Australia says H5 bird flu strain likely spreading locally after 11 new suspected cases identified No H5 bird flu has been detected in commercial poultry in the state or elsewhere in the country Amir Latif Arain 29 July 2026 • Update : 29 July 2026 KARAÇI Authorities in South Australia said Wednesday that it is "very, very likely" that the H5 strain of bird flu is spreading locally after the state identified 11 more suspected cases, local media reported. Of the cases, seven were found in the state's South East and four on Kangaroo Island, the Australian Broadcasting Corporation reported. While officials are still waiting for final testing results, South Australia's Primary Industries Minister Clare Scriven said it was "extremely likely" to represent "localized distribution" of the virus. The state government expects results from a Commonwealth Scientific and Industrial Research Organization (CSIRO) testing lab Wednesday or Thursday to verify whether the disease is spreading locally between wild birds. "What we've seen around the world is we can't stop H5 bird flu, and there has of course in every other continent around the world been local transmission," Scriven said. Authorities have temporarily closed Seal Bay to the public to help protect the local sea lion population. Scriven said there has been no detections of H5 bird flu in commercial poultry across the state or the rest of the country. South Australia's first confirmed case of H5 bird flu was in a giant petrel found on June 14. Location and population
Region: South Australia. Specific areas: South East (7 cases) and Kangaroo Island (4 cases). Affected population: Wild birds. No commercial poultry affected.
Event
South Australian authorities reported 11 new suspected cases of H5 bird flu strain, stating it is "very, very likely" spreading locally. The cases were identified in wild birds, with no detections in commercial poultry. The event is preliminary, awaiting final testing results.
Scale, impact and risk
Cases: 11 new suspected cases identified. Deaths: Not reported. Exposures: Not reported. Spread: Likely localized transmission between wild birds. Health-system effects: Not reported. Operational effects: Seal Bay temporarily closed to protect sea lion population. Risk: Officials stated it is "extremely likely" to represent localized distribution.
Response, status and uncertainty
Investigation: Ongoing, with final testing results expected from CSIRO lab on Wednesday or Thursday. Testing: Pending confirmation from CSIRO lab. Isolation: Not reported. Contact tracing: Not applicable. Prevention: Seal Bay closed to public. Treatment: Not reported. Official communication: Primary Industries Minister Clare Scriven provided statements. Current status: Preliminary, awaiting confirmation. Unresolved information: Whether the disease is spreading locally between wild birds. Source-body limitations: None, as content_status is full.
